个人生活

PERSONAL JOURNAL · 7 stories

A1

“周日恐慌”真实存在——而且愈演愈烈

P1 · 必读

Ray A. Smith

随着工作量增加、职业保障减弱，人们越来越难遏制周一前的恐惧螺旋

As workloads mount, people struggle to tame the pre-Monday dread spiral

吉尔·戴维斯的工作周正式从周一早上开始。但实际上，从每个周日下午开始，她就会感到焦虑，各种不安的想法开始涌上心头，就像一个定时的闹钟。

“我是否已经做好充分的准备，并且有条不紊？我是否已经为这次会议做好了准备？”戴维斯说道。她是一家非营利组织的首席发展官。为了驱散这种焦虑，她经常开始工作，写邮件并将其保存为草稿，这样就不会打扰她的同事。很可能，她的同事们也已经开始为工作感到焦虑。

“周日恐慌”已经成为美国人周末生活中不可或缺的一部分，以至于《韦氏词典》上个月将其添加到在线词典中。它的定义是：一种焦虑或悲伤的感觉，当周末即将结束时，人们可能会感到这种感觉，原因可能是工作/学习周即将开始，或者没有更多的时间进行有趣的或放松的周末活动，或者两者兼而有之。

这种对周一的恐惧并非一种新的现象，但有理由相信，随着工作量的增加和工作保障的减少，这种恐惧正在加剧。根据招聘平台FlexJobs对2100人进行的10月份调查，超过一半的美国工人表示他们会经历“周日恐慌”，而最常见的诱因是未完成的任务清单。

很快，这种焦虑感就会蔓延到周六，使周六也变成了一个令人感到焦虑的日子，因为人们会开始为周日将要感受到的焦虑而感到不安。

对于戴维斯来说，这种焦虑感已经严重影响了她的周日，以至于她买了一个30美元的沙漏来控制这种情绪。沙子流完需要一个小时——这是她现在允许自己工作或甚至思考工作的时间上限。在一个最近的周日，“当沙子流完时，我站起来，离开了房间，去了另一个房间，”她说。

在不产生负面副作用的情况下，有效地缓解“周日恐慌”是一项微妙的平衡艺术。它需要人们保持一定的忙碌程度，以减轻压力，但不能是那种会拖累所有人的忙碌。看来我们在这方面做得越来越差。

根据劳动力分析公司ActivTrak为《华尔街日报》提供的数据，去年员工用于工作消息和聊天的时间占周日的约12%，而2023年为6.6%。

“人们觉得他们必须更加努力地工作，以确保自己不会被解雇，”《Scary Good：发现摆脱周日恐慌的生活》一书的作者、一位高管教练安吉·卡伦说道。她的建议是什么？诚实地问自己，为什么要在周日处理工作。这样做是否能带来积极的回报？“你不必屈服于周日恐慌，”卡伦说。“我们让星期一毁掉了我们的星期天。停止。与其接受这种默认状态，不如活在当下。”

一位在科技行业工作多年的高管乔安娜·约翰斯顿过去会通过早上 6 点起床，挤出一个小时的时间来为星期一做准备，然后在晚上再投入更多时间，以此来对抗她的周日恐慌。当她发送 Slack 和其他消息时，通常是发给同事或回复经理。但她并不总是遵守与下属进行周末沟通的不成文规定。“我确信，在我变得更加敏感，意识到这种行为会造成干扰之前，我也曾对我的直接下属和更广泛的团队这样做，”约翰斯顿说。她于 6 月从公司职位退休，现在担任高管教练。尽管职业生涯发生了转变，她仍然在周日工作。“我想我只是有问题，”她笑着说。

几年前，克里斯·凯勒经营自己的营销公司，经常一周工作七天。现在，他担任公司职位，他将自己限制在周日清晨的两个小时内，为新的一周做准备。否则，“我知道情况会迅速失控，”凯勒说，他是信用合作社的营销主管。凯勒说，他通常会利用这段时间，将笔记口述给转录应用程序，为会议做准备。“如果我不把想法和思路表达出来，我就无法享受周末的其他活动，”他说。

精神科医生是否认可这种做法？如果周日做一些工作可以减轻你在周一的焦虑或不知所措的感觉，那么这样做是可以的，但要设定一些限制，克利夫兰诊所行为健康部门的精神科医生基娅-拉伊·普雷维特说。“如果我能快速登录并回复这封电子邮件，那就是我所要做的，”她说。“你必须设定界限，并说：‘我不会回复其他电子邮件。’”

其他人建议尝试所有方法，以避免完全进行工作。考虑在周五多花一些时间在工作上，“这样你就可以正式完成工作”，或者周一早点到公司，“以免一开局就处于被动”，工业组织心理学家艾米·库珀·哈基姆说。“总有一种方法可以调整这些额外的时间。”

如果所有方法都失败了，可以尝试重新定义。吉尔·戴维斯，这位经常在周日下午感到焦虑的人，与一位高管教练合作，帮助她将“周日恐慌”重新定义为“也许是周一”。这个想法是，要以好奇的态度来迎接周一，而不是认为一切都必须在那时完成并达到完美。“我试图减轻那种压力，即一切都必须在周一之前完成，”她说。“我周日晚上睡得更好。”

Jill Davis's official work week starts Monday morning. Unofficially, it kicks off every Sunday afternoon when, like clockwork, anxious thoughts begin seeping into her brain.

"Am I organized and ready enough? Am I prepared for this meeting?" says Davis, the chief development officer at a nonprofit. To bat the angst away, she'll often launch into work, writing emails and saving them as drafts so she doesn't disturb her colleagues. Chances are they're already fretting about work, too.

"Sunday scaries" have become such a fixture of Americans' weekends that Merriam-Webster added the term to its online dictionary last month. Its definition: a feeling of anxiety or sadness one may feel when the weekend is almost over, either because the work/school week is about to begin, there is no more time for fun or relaxing weekend activities, or both.

That pre-Monday dread isn't a new phenomenon, but there's reason to believe it's intensifying as workloads mount and job security ebbs. More than half of U.S. workers say they experience

[Continued on page A12.]

the Sunday scaries, according to an October survey of 2,100 people by job platform FlexJobs. Having a list of unfinished tasks hanging over them was the most commonly cited cause.

It's only a matter of time before the scaries ruin Saturday too, turning it into a day to dread the dread we'll feel on Sunday.

For Davis, the jitters so consumed her Sundays that she bought a $30 hourglass to rein them in. It takes an hour for the sand to run through it—the maximum time she now lets herself work, or even think about work. On a recent Sunday, “when it ran out, I got up and I left and went in the other room,” she said.

Effectively treating the Sunday scaries without causing negative side effects is a delicate balancing act. It requires being busy enough to ease the stress but not the kind of busy that drags everyone down. It appears we're getting worse at that.

The share of Sundays that employees devoted to work messaging and chats was about 12% last year, compared with 6.6% in 2023, according to data compiled for The Wall Street Journal by workforce analytics firm ActivTrak.

“People feel like they have to toil away harder in order to make sure they don't get fired,” said Angie Callen, an executive coach and author of “Scary Good: Discovering Life Beyond the Sunday Scaries.” Her advice? Ask yourself honestly why you are engaging with work on a Sunday. Will you get a positive return on your time? “You don't have to settle for the Sunday scaries,” said Callen. “We let Mondays ruin our Sundays. Stop. Be present in your life instead of accepting that default.”

Joanna Johnston, a longtime tech executive, used to combat her Sunday scaries by getting up as early as 6 a.m. to squeeze in an hour of Monday prep work, then putting in more time in the evenings. When she sent Slack and other messages, it was usually to a peer or in response to a manager. But she didn't always adhere to the unwritten rules of weekend communication with subordinates. “I'm sure before I became more sensitive to the intrusion this caused, I did it to my direct reports and broader team as well,” said Johnston, who retired from corporate roles in June and now coaches executives. Despite the career shift, she's still working on Sundays. “I think I just have a problem,” she laughed.

Until a few years ago, Chris Keller ran his own marketing agency and often worked seven days a week. Now in a corporate role, he limits himself to two hours on early Sunday mornings to prepare for the week. Otherwise, “I know it could get away from me really quickly,” said Keller, marketing chief at a credit union. Keller said he will typically use the time to dictate notes to a transcription app to prepare for meetings. “If I don't get ideas and thoughts out, I'll not be able to enjoy the other things that I'm supposed to be doing on the weekends,” he said.

Do psychiatrists approve? If doing some work on Sunday will relieve you of feeling behind or overwhelmed on Monday, it's OK, just set guardrails, said Kia-Rai Prewitt, a psychiatrist with Cleveland Clinic's Behavioral Health. “If I can just log in really quick and respond to this email, that's all I'm going to do,” she said. “You have to set the boundary and say, ‘I'm not going to respond to other emails.’”

Others recommend exhausting all options to avoid work altogether. Consider spending a little more time at work on Friday “so you can officially be done” or getting to work a little earlier on Monday “so that you're not going to be behind the eight ball,” said Amy Cooper Hakim, an industrial organizational psychologist. “There's always a way to shift those same extra minutes.”

If all else fails, try a rebrand. Davis, the Sunday afternoon worrier, worked with an executive coach who helped her reimagine the “Sunday scaries” as “Monday maybe.” The idea is to approach Monday with curiosity rather than thinking everything has to be buttoned-up and perfect by then. “I'm trying to release the pressure of what's so special about everything getting done before Monday,” she said. “I sleep better on Sunday nights.”

背景资料 · CONTEXT原文有

招聘平台 FlexJobs 10 月对 2100 人的调查显示，超过半数美国员工会经历“周日恐慌”，最常见的原因是未完成的工作清单。劳动力分析公司 ActivTrak 的数据显示，员工周日用于工作消息和聊天的时间占比从 2023 年的 6.6% 升至去年的约 12%。精神科医生建议设定界限（如只回一封邮件），组织心理学家则建议把额外工作挪到周五或周一清晨。

A11

想拿下明年夏天那份 5 万美元的实习？已经太晚了。

P2 · 重点

Callum Borchers (On the Clock)

顶尖的金融公司开始更早地进行招聘，并为有潜力的优秀人才提供丰厚的报酬。

Elite financial firms are recruiting earlier and paying handsomely for prospective talent

学年才刚开始一个月，但对于那些希望在明年夏天获得一份顶尖实习机会的大学生来说，可能已经太晚了。那些提供为期10周的实习，并支付约5万美元报酬的顶尖金融公司，已经开始招聘，甚至已经持续了数周或数月。当大多数学生还在装饰宿舍时，那些积极进取的大学生正在争夺这些令人垂涎的实习机会。有些人已经获得了2027年的实习机会，或者正在深入面试阶段。正如学生们彼此之间展开激烈竞争一样，城堡证券、Five Rings和简街资本等公司也在努力争取，希望在竞争对手之前锁定最有前途的人才。在这场人才争夺战中，20岁左右的年轻人获得的薪资水平，甚至超过了大多数美国人在职业生涯巅峰时期所获得的薪资，更不用说实习生的薪资了。

卡尔蒂克·卡拉姆说：“当我接到实习机会的电话，他们告诉我薪资是多少时，我问了好几次，确认他们说的是正确的数字。”他刚刚在萨斯奎汉纳国际集团完成了量化交易实习。“我无法想象实习生应该获得这么多钱。”

顶尖的实习项目一直以来都竞争激烈，但现在那些提供超高薪酬的实习机会，录取率甚至低于1%。你可能比进入哈佛大学更容易。结果就是，申请周期越来越早。现在有些公司在当前的实习生还在工作时，就开始接受下一年夏季实习的申请。尽早开始，可以为多轮面试争取时间。这些面试最终会以现场的“超级面试日”结束，评估人员会判断候选人本人是否如屏幕上那般出色——现场可是用不了 AI 的。

最挑剔的金融公司通常在寻找一种无法在学生的成绩单上体现的原始智慧。他们有时会关注大学的国际象棋和扑克锦标赛，寻找那些在玩概率游戏和预测未来事件方面表现出惊人能力的人。卡拉姆在两项游戏中都表现出色。他说，他认为他在扑克牌桌上的能力，比他在俄亥俄州立大学获得的优异成绩，更能提升他的实习申请。他在那里学习计算机科学和统计学。此外，还有量化因素。在量化金融领域，许多薪酬最高的实习机会都集中于此，经验的重要性可能不如其他大多数学科。

如果你要选择一位个人理财顾问，你会希望他拥有数十年的判断力和直觉——一位经验丰富、处事沉稳的专业人士，因为他已经见识过各种情况。量化交易员不一定需要这些。他们使用数学模型来预测市场走向，并以在投资决策中消除情绪为荣。“他们可以因为他们的技术技能和我们给予他们的机会，而迅速变得非常出色，”千禧管理的全球人才招聘负责人约翰·塔拉里科说，但他补充说，经验也是一个优势。在这种情况下，为利基金融职位寻找有前途的人才，有点像寻找运动员。一位大学四分卫可能不知道如何解读 NFL 的防守策略，或者没有在职业球队的比赛风格中获得任何经验。然而，教练可能会看到他手臂力量和决策能力方面的潜力。同样，像千禧管理这样的公司，可能会看到那些从未上过金融课程的候选人身上存在的潜力。如果他们的逻辑思维、解决问题的能力和模式识别能力都非常出色，他们就可能成为大把赚钱的交易员。为了确定他们是否具备这些能力，值得为他们支付丰厚的报酬，让他们参加为期 10 周的“训练营”。在这个级别，实习生不是去端咖啡，而是为了获得全职工作机会，这些工作的基础工资约为 30 万美元。由于风险如此之大，不可避免地会出现一些不端行为。

一位前Arrowstreet资本的实习生告诉我，对于那些申请顶尖金融实习机会的学生来说，他们的非官方座右铭是“总是说你还是个大三学生”。原因是，三年级的大学生最有吸引力，因为他们即将毕业。大二和大一的学生有时会虚报年级，希望以此提高机会。

我一直以为大学生只是为了去酒吧而撒谎。

他们的欺骗行为充满了绝望。许多成绩优异的学生对落后于人有着不健康的恐惧，达特茅斯职业设计中心的执行董事乔·卡特里诺说。“这对我们来说是一个令人担忧的问题，因为我们看到学生们有这种焦虑和压力，他们必须弄清楚自己想做什么，但他们才刚到这里，”他说。在常春藤和其他顶尖大学，这通常会导致学生们在一年级新生入学指导结束后，几乎立即开始努力争取进入校园内具有选择性的俱乐部。加入这些俱乐部可以为简历增添亮点，并可以接触到同行和校友网络。伊山·沙阿回忆说，他在宾夕法尼亚大学的第一学期，被量化交易俱乐部拒绝了。他在第二学期再次申请，但再次被拒绝。第三次，他终于成功了。加入量化交易俱乐部，帮助他获得了在萨斯奎汉纳的实习机会，当时他还是一个即将升入大四的学生。但他没有收到毕业后继续工作的邀请。“仅仅因为你获得了一个实习机会，并且看到了那笔丰厚的薪水，并不意味着你已经成功了，”他说。这种失望并没有持续太久；沙阿几周前从另一家公司获得了一份全职工作。尽管如此，他的大起大落凸显了这些超竞争实习项目有多么不稳定。即使是极擅长预测的人，最稳的赌注也是：你迟早会被教做人。

The school year is barely a month old, but for college students hoping to land a primo internship next summer it might already be too late. Elite financial firms that pay around $50,000 for 10-week programs have been recruiting for weeks, if not months. While most of their peers are still decorating their dorm rooms, hard-charging undergrads are vying for these coveted seats. Some already have offers for 2027 or are deep in the interview process. And just as students are in a frenzied competition with one another, firms such as Citadel, Five Rings and Jane Street Capital are hustling to lock down the most promising talent before their rivals do. In this arms race, 20-year-olds now command pay rates that most Americans don't reach at the peaks of their careers, never mind as interns.

“When I got the offer call and they said how much the salary was, I asked a few times if they were saying the right number,” says Karthik Kallam, fresh off a quantitative-trading internship at Susquehanna International Group. “I can't fathom how an intern should be making that much money.”

Top internship programs have long been competitive, but those dangling stratospheric compensation rates now have acceptance rates of less than 1%. You'd have a better chance of getting into Harvard. The result is that application cycles keep starting earlier. Some firms now open applications for the following summer's internships while the current cohort is still on the job. Starting early affords time for multiple rounds of interviews. These culminate in on-site “super-days” where evaluators gauge whether candidates are as impressive in person as they seemed on-screen—and don't have access to AI.

The most selective financial firms are often looking for a kind of raw intelligence that doesn't show up on students' transcripts. They sometimes scout collegiate chess and poker tournaments, looking for people who are uncannily good at playing the odds and anticipating what will happen next. Kallam plays both games competitively. He says he believes his prowess at the table did more to boost his internship application than his sparkling GPA at Ohio State, where he is studying computer science and statistics. Then there is the quant factor. In quantitative finance, where many of the highest-paying internships are concentrated, experience arguably matters less than in most other disciplines.

If you were choosing a personal-financial planner, you'd want someone with decades' worth of judgment and intuition—a pro with a steady demeanor that comes from having seen it all before. Quant traders don't necessarily need that. They use mathematical models to predict where markets are going and take pride in removing emotion from investment decisions. “They can become exceptional very quickly because of their technical skills and the opportunities we give them,” says John Talarico, global head of talent acquisition at Millennium Management, though he adds that experience is a plus. In this way, finding promising talent for niche finance roles is a bit like scouting athletes. A college quarterback may not know how to read an NFL defense or have any experience in a professional team's style of play. Yet coaches might see potential in his arm strength and decision-making. Similarly, firms like Millennium might see an upside in candidates who have never taken a finance course. If their logic, problem solving and pattern recognition are off the charts, then they could become major moneymakers. To find out whether they have what it takes, it's worth paying them handsomely for the 10-week equivalent of training camp. At this level, interns aren't fetching coffee; they are auditioning for full-time jobs with base salaries around $300,000. With so much at stake, shenanigans are inevitable.

“Always say you're a junior” is the unofficial motto of students applying for top-flight financial internships, a former Arrowstreet Capital intern told me. The thinking is that third-year college students are the most appealing because they are close to graduation. Sophomores and freshmen sometimes fudge their class years in hopes of improving their chances.

And I thought college students only lied about their ages to get into bars.

Their deception is laced with desperation. A lot of high-achieving students have an unhealthy fear of falling behind, says Joe Catrino, executive director of the Dartmouth Center for Career Design. “It's a troubling thing for us because we see students that have this anxiety and stress that they have to figure out what they want to do, and they just got here,” he says. At Ivy and other top colleges, this often results in a frenzied effort to gain acceptance into selective clubs on campus almost as soon as first-year orientation wraps up. Membership promises a standout résumé line, as well as access to peer and alumni networks. Ishaan Shah recalls being rejected by the quantitative-trading club at Penn in his first semester of college. He applied again in the spring semester—and was turned down once more. The third time was the charm. Being in the quant club helped propel him to an internship at Susquehanna last summer as a rising senior. But he didn't receive an offer to return after graduation. “Just because you've got an internship offer at one place and have seen that big paycheck, it doesn't mean you've made it,” he says. The disappointment didn't last long; Shah landed a full-time job offer from another firm a couple of weeks ago. Still, his highs and lows underscore how precarious these ultracompetitive internship programs can be. Even for those who are highly skilled at making predictions, the surest bet is that you'll be humbled eventually.

A11

尼古丁品牌形象焕然一新。

P2 · 重点

Sara Ashley O'Brien

以下内容最初发表在莎拉·阿什利·奥布莱恩的每周《Wellness newsletter》中。

The following items first appeared in Sara Ashley O'Brien's weekly Wellness newsletter

尼古丁的品牌重塑正在如火如荼地进行。网红和企业家们正在传播一种观点，即这种具有高度成瘾性的物质，在少量使用时实际上对人体有益。他们声称，它可以改善注意力、改善情绪和增强记忆力。推广无烟尼古丁产品的公司也在积极利用健康、保健和健身的概念。

Lucy公司销售“智能尼古丁”产品，包括调味口香糖和尼古丁袋，在其网站上表示：“无论您使用尼古丁来提高注意力、增强精力还是放松身心，都有适合您的Lucy产品。”Athletic Nicotine的网站上展示了一位年轻女性在划船机上运动，配文为：“为我们每个人体内的赢家提供更高水平的尼古丁。”另一家品牌Nic Nac，宣传其产品含有“优质成分”，并告诉消费者，可以在任何需要提高表现的场合使用其尼古丁含片，例如在健身房、办公室或飞机上。

这些公司都告诉我，他们的产品是为现有的成年尼古丁使用者设计的。Athletic Nicotine表示，他们不会将自己定位为“健康产品、保健补充剂或性能增强剂”。Lucy表示，其网站上的文案旨在吸引那些已经将尼古丁用于这些目的的人。

引言：“网红们正在传播一种观点，即尼古丁在少量使用时实际上是有益的。研究驳斥了这些说法。”

多年来，网红们一直在谈论尼古丁作为一种保健产品。他们推广了无烟尼古丁配方，包括尼古丁含片、尼古丁袋和口香糖。这在DIY健康时代是一种常见的现象：网红们利用轶事证据和有限的研究来推广下一款“神奇”产品。他们的宣传可能会对其追随者产生强大的影响。虽然尼古丁袋这类产品被认为比香烟危害小——香烟几乎损害人体的每一个器官——但正是尼古丁让烟草产品如此容易上瘾。

肺科医生哈斯米娜·卡图里亚最近在《华盛顿邮报》撰文，驳斥了尼古丁的养生说法，并强调了其风险。她写道，尼古丁会激活人体的“战斗或逃跑”反应，增加心率和血压，并可能扰乱睡眠。

弗吉尼亚大学（我的母校）公共卫生科学副教授梅丽莎·利特尔告诉我，研究人员尚未发现认知能力与尼古丁使用之间存在关联。她同时也是该校尼古丁与烟草研究中心的负责人。她说：“关于尼古丁对记忆、情绪和新陈代谢的益处，现有证据并不充分。”她补充说，从尼古丁使用中获得的情绪改善，主要来自“使用尼古丁替代疗法来缓解戒断症状的吸烟者。大多数不吸烟的人发现尼古丁会使他们的情绪恶化，甚至会增加恶心感。”此外，她表示，尼古丁袋这类产品的长期健康影响仍然未知。

与此同时，从2023年1月到2026年5月，尼古丁袋的销量增加了319%。

The rebranding of nicotine is well underway. Influencers and entrepreneurs are spreading the idea that the highly addictive substance can actually be good for you in small doses. They're claiming it can improve attention span, mood and memory. Companies promoting tobacco-free nicotine products are leaning in to health, wellness and fitness associations, too.

Lucy, which sells “intelligent nicotine” products, including flavored gum and pouches, states on its website that “whether you use nicotine to enhance focus, boost your energy, or relax—there's a Lucy perfect for you.” Athletic Nicotine's website features a young woman on a rowing machine with text that reads: “Next-level nicotine for the winner in all of us.” Another brand, Nic Nac, touts “premium ingredients” and tells consumers to use its lozenges “anywhere you need an edge, whether it's in the gym, office, or on an airplane.”

The companies each told me that their products are intended for existing adult nicotine consumers. Athletic Nicotine said it does not market itself “as a health product, wellness supplement, or performance enhancer.” Lucy said that its website copy is meant to appeal to people who are already using nicotine for those purposes.

Pull quote: “Influencers are spreading the idea nicotine can actually be good in small doses. Research debunks claims.”

For years, influencers have been talking about nicotine as a wellness product. They've promoted smoke-free nicotine formulations including lozenges, pouches and gums. It's a familiar phenomenon in the era of D.I.Y. health: Influencers using anecdotal evidence and limited research to peddle the next holy-grail product. Their talking points can have powerful ripple effects for their followings. While nicotine products like pouches are deemed less harmful than cigarettes—which harm nearly every organ of the body—the substance is what makes tobacco products so addictive.

The pulmonologist Dr. Hasmeena Kathuria penned a column in the Washington Post recently debunking wellness claims about nicotine and underscoring its risks. She writes that nicotine activates the body's fight-or-flight response, increases heart rate and blood pressure and can disrupt sleep.

Researchers haven't found an association between cognition and nicotine use, Melissa Little, an associate professor of public-health sciences at the University of Virginia—my alma mater—told me. She's also director of the school's Center for Nicotine & Tobacco Research. “The evidence on the benefits of nicotine on memory, mood and metabolism is not well supported,” she said. She added that any mood improvements findings from nicotine use are primarily from “smokers who use nicotine replacement to manage withdrawal symptoms. Most nonsmokers find nicotine worsens their mood and even increases nausea.” Plus, the long-term health effects of nicotine products like pouches are still unknown, she said.

Meanwhile, sales of nicotine pouches increased by 319% between January 2023 and May 2026.

A11

面部脂肪移植术的需求正在增长。

P2 · 重点

Sara Ashley O'Brien

摘自莎拉·阿什利·奥布莱恩每周健康通讯。

Item from Sara Ashley O'Brien's weekly Wellness newsletter

我的同事查维·利伯和我最近发表了一篇文章，讨论了面部填充剂为何不再受欢迎。这种填充剂带来的效果——过度丰满的嘴唇和饱满的脸颊——正在失去吸引力。这部分原因是人们因为使用GLP-1而体重减轻，因此他们的面部特征也需要相应地调整。

美国整形外科医师协会最近发布了年度报告，其中包含2025年美容手术趋势的统计数据。值得注意的是，该协会82%的会员外科医生表示，在2025年收到了与GLP-1使用相关的咨询请求。在所有追踪的美容手术中，同比增长最显著的是面部脂肪移植。

2025年面部脂肪移植同比增长39%，这种手术包括通过抽脂术从身体其他部位（如腹部）提取脂肪，然后将其注射到面部。其目的是为了恢复面部年轻状态，并补充因体重减轻而流失的体积。人们开始服用这些药物时，通常并不希望出现“Ozempic脸”的效果。他们希望达到的效果是更自然、更饱满的面部轮廓。

“人们希望看起来和感觉一样好，”美国整形外科医师协会主席、休斯顿整形外科医生鲍勃·巴苏医生告诉我。“人们希望看起来焕发活力，但他们希望看起来像真实的自己，我认为脂肪在其中发挥了作用。”

美国整形外科医师协会表示，他们不再报告手术的费用，因为这些费用会因多种因素而异，包括地理位置和外科医生的专业水平。2024年，该协会表示，面部脂肪移植手术的医生费用在3000美元到5500美元之间，不包括麻醉、手术室费用和其他费用。

凯利·基林医生是美国整形外科医师协会的会员，也是比弗利山庄Practice Healthcare的整形外科医生。她告诉我，面部脂肪移植有时甚至可以在诊所进行，具体取决于需要填充的脂肪量以及是否需要进行其他手术。她说，手术费用可能从4000美元到20000美元以上不等，具体取决于是否需要进行其他项目，例如皮肤紧致手术。

如需订阅每周五的新闻简报，请访问wsj.com/newsletters。

My colleague Chavie Lieber and I published an article recently about how facial filler has fallen out of favor. The filler look—characterized by overly plump lips and pillowy cheeks—is losing appeal. It's partly because people are shrinking due to GLP-1s, so their features, too, must scale to size.

The American Society of Plastic Surgeons recently released its annual report with statistics showing cosmetic-procedure trends in 2025. Notably, 82% of the organization's member surgeons reported receiving consultation requests related to GLP-1 use in 2025. Of all cosmetic procedures tracked, one saw the most growth year-over-year: facial fat grafting.

Facial fat grafting, up 39% in 2025 compared with the year prior, involves removing fat from elsewhere in the body, such as the stomach, via liposuction and then having it injected into the face. The idea is to rejuvenate and restore lost volume, which might come as a result of weight loss. “Ozempic Face” isn't what most people are going for when they start taking these medications. The desired outcome is a more naturally filled out look.

“People want to look as good as they feel,” ASPS president Dr. Bob Basu, a plastic surgeon in Houston, told me. “People want to look refreshed, but they want to look like an authentic version of themselves, and I think that's where fat comes into play.”

ASPS said it no longer reports the costs of procedures because they're variable, fluctuating by many factors including geographic location or a surgeon's expertise. In 2024, the organization said the price for the surgeon's fee for facial fat grafting was between $3,000 and $5,500, which doesn't include additional costs for things like anesthesia, operating room facility fees and other fees.

Dr. Kelly Killeen, an ASPS member and plastic surgeon at the Practice Healthcare in Beverly Hills, told me facial fat grafting can even sometimes be done in the office, depending on how much fat is being placed and any other procedures involved. She said it could run from as little as $4,000 up to more than $20,000, with add-ons like skin-tightening procedures.

To subscribe to the Friday newsletter, go to wsj.com/newsletters.

A12

人们分享自己还债过程的视频，引发了广泛关注。

P2 · 重点

Dalvin Brown

观众回来为他们加油、评判，看余额是涨是跌。

Viewers return to cheer, judge and see whether balances went up or down

面对自己的债务可能会让人感到痛苦。观看别人处理自己的债务可能会让人感到引人入胜。罗德尼·摩根在七月份就意识到了这一点，当时他发布了一段TikTok视频，视频中他坐在一个充气床垫上，头靠在一只手上，屏幕上显示了他的债务总额为40,058.44美元。

“我正从零开始重做空少，如实公开我的起点，”33岁的洛杉矶居民摩根写道。这与他平时发的空少生活内容不同，并且获得了150万次观看——远远超过了他之前表现最好的视频所获得的约10万次观看。

TikTok上充斥着各种各样的网红，他们提供各种关于如何改善财务状况的建议，无论是为了早日退休而储蓄，还是为了获得被动收入，或者利用信用卡积分来资助环游世界的旅行。但用户也在寻找能够吸引大量观众的内容，这些内容展示了许多人可能因为感到尴尬而不愿分享的个人财务困境。

这种形式很简单：用户分享他们欠款的确切金额，以及他们如何逐步偿还债务——或者遭遇挫折的最新情况。观众观看这些数字的增减，并为这些人加油助威，通常还会提供一些建议。

根据Tubular Labs的数据，在截至9月份的12个月里，超过28,000个与债务相关的视频被上传到TikTok。Tubular Labs负责跟踪社交视频的观看情况。

#DebtTok之所以吸引人，部分原因在于它涉及了一个禁忌话题，人们觉得即使是与朋友和家人也无法讨论。康奈尔大学研究社交媒体文化的教授布鲁克·艾琳·达菲说，就像八卦一样，这些视频让观众得以评判他人的财务选择，并拿自己的做比较。

苏茜·欧曼和戴夫·拉姆齐等个人理财专家，通过向那些分享自己财务问题的人提供建议，获得了大量的追随者。TikTok视频则省略了这位“专家”——视频的主角是面临问题的人，而不是提供建议的人。

移动银行应用Chime的注册理财规划师布兰妮·卡斯特罗说，观众看的是英雄之旅式的戏剧张力，就像电影里有人挑战巨大障碍。“人们渴望看到这个人取得成功，这是一种非常人性化的情感，”她说。

对于那些发布关于自己债务的视频的人来说，获得大量的观看次数可以帮助他们偿还账单。TikTok会向创作者支付一些热门视频的费用，并且观众可以在直播期间发送虚拟礼物。当品牌赞助创作者的帖子时，或者当追随者通过他们分享的链接进行购买时，创作者还可以获得收入。

例如，在摩根的视频走红之后，一家个人理财应用程序向他提供了迄今为止他最大的社交媒体合作机会。他预计这次赞助的帖子可以帮助他偿还一部分债务：即他用于改造房屋的Home Depot和Lowe's信用卡上剩余的约800美元。

蒂安娜·卡特每天晚上睡觉前都会录制一段关于她债务情况的更新视频，有时甚至是在晚上 10 点，地点就在她的厨房里。“我每天都期待这一刻，”她说。卡特是一位 31 岁的公共学校区人力资源总监，一开始并没有透露她欠款的具体金额：54,057.93 美元。之后，她决定公开这个数字。“人们更容易理解，也能更好地与我的情况产生共鸣，”居住在宾夕法尼亚州哈里斯堡的卡特说道。她的粉丝并不总是认同她的选择，比如她在个人形象维护上的开销。“你还去做头发，我还怎么监督你还债？”一位评论者写道。卡特表示，她将去美发店视为固定支出，并在其他方面进行削减，比如自带午餐去上班。她将通过发布视频获得的收入用于支付托儿费用和偿还债务。

凯拉·罗斯在大学期间，她的父亲给她一张信用卡，用于购买食品和汽油。结果她用这张信用卡购买了衣服、运动装备和家居装饰品，并在她的视频中推广了一些她购买的商品。现在，凯拉·罗斯是一位 29 岁的全职网红，居住在佛罗里达州棕榈滩。她在三月份告诉她的粉丝，她信用卡欠款超过 25,000 美元，并计划用她通过视频获得的收入来偿还。关于她债务的帖子吸引了新的粉丝，她也在八月份还清了信用卡欠款。“正是这些你们现在正在观看的、有点傻气的视频，帮助我偿还了信用卡债务，”她告诉她的粉丝。

Facing your debt can be painful. Watching someone else deal with theirs can be gripping. That became clear to Rodney Morgan in July, when he posted a TikTok of himself sitting on a blowup mattress with his head in one hand, and his debt tally of $40,058.44 imposed on the screen.

“I'm rebuilding from scratch as a flight attendant and being honest about where I'm starting,” wrote Morgan, a 33-year-old Los Angeles resident. It was a changeup from his usual posts about life as a flight attendant, and racked up 1.5 million views—far more than the roughly 100,000 views he said his previous best-performing videos drew.

TikTok is filled with influencers dispensing all manner of advice on how to get ahead financially, whether it is saving to retire early, earning a passive income or using credit-card rewards to fund a globe-trotting lifestyle. But users are also finding big audiences showing the private money struggles many would be too embarrassed to share.

The format is simple: Users share the exact amount they owe, along with updates on how they are chipping away at the figure—or suffering setbacks. Viewers tune in to see the numbers move up and down and root for people, often weighing in with advice.

More than 28,000 debt-related videos were uploaded to TikTok in the 12 months through September, according to Tubular Labs, which tracks social-video viewing.

Part of #DebtTok's draw is that it addresses a taboo topic that people feel they can't discuss even with friends and family. Like gossip, the videos let viewers size up other people's financial choices and measure their own against them, said Brooke Erin Duffy, a Cornell University professor who studies social-media culture.

Personal-finance gurus like Suze Orman and Dave Ramsey gained huge followings by dispensing advice to callers who shared their money problems. TikTok videos cut out the guru—the host is the person with the problem, not the one giving advice.

Viewers tune in for the drama of a hero's journey, like a movie where someone takes on a big obstacle, said Brittney Castro, a certified financial planner at Chime, a mobile banking app. “It's so human to want to see that person win and succeed,” she said.

For those posting about their debt, racking up views can help pay off bills. TikTok pays creators for some popular videos, and viewers can send virtual gifts during livestreams. Creators also earn income when brands sponsor their posts, or a cut when followers buy through links they share.

After Morgan's video took off, for instance, a personal-finance app offered him his biggest social-media deal yet. He expects the sponsored post to pay off a piece of his debt: the roughly $800 left on the Home Depot and Lowe's cards he opened to remodel his house.

Every night before bed, Tianna Carter records an update on her debt, sometimes at 10 p.m. in her kitchen. “I look forward to it every day,” she said. Carter, a 31-year-old human-resources director for a public school district, didn't say at first the amount she owed: $54,057.93. Then she decided to share the figure. “People understand more and can relate more to my number,” said Carter, who lives in Harrisburg, Pa. Her followers don't always agree with her choices, such as her spending on her personal upkeep. “How am I supposed to keep you accountable if you're getting your hair done?” one commenter wrote. Carter said she treats salon visits as fixed costs and cuts elsewhere, like bringing lunch to work. She puts the money she makes from her posts toward daycare costs and paying down her debt.

When Kayla Rose was in college, her father gave her a credit card for groceries and gas. She ended up using it to buy clothes, workout gear and home décor as well, promoting some of what she bought in her videos. Rose, now a 29-year-old full-time influencer in Palm Beach, Fla., told followers in March that she owed more than $25,000 on credit cards and planned to pay it off with money she made from her videos. The posts about her debt brought new followers, and she paid off the cards in August. “What paid off my credit-card debt was these silly little videos you guys are currently watching,” she told followers.

体育

SPORTS · 4 stories

A18

关键时刻的失误导致得克萨斯队失利

P2 · 重点

Robert O'Connell

排名第一的得克萨斯长角牛队在四分卫阿奇·曼宁比赛末段出现失误后，输给了俄克拉荷马捷足者队。

The No. 1 Longhorns lost to Oklahoma after a late wobble by quarterback Arch Manning

仅仅一个月前，阿奇·曼宁终于展现出了他作为足球界翘首以盼的明星四分卫应有的风采。凭借第四节的疯狂反扑，曼宁率队完成20分大逆转，战胜俄亥俄州立大学，并帮助球队登上“前25强”榜首。他巩固了自己作为海斯曼奖热门的地位，带领得克萨斯队争取全国冠军，并确立自己作为NFL选秀状元的地位。

但在周六对阵俄克拉荷马捷足者队的比赛中，曼宁遭遇了令人震惊的失利，这场比赛表明，尽管他被誉为足球界未来的明星，但他离成为一名成熟的球员还有很长的路要走。

在比赛还剩20秒，双方比分持平时，曼宁试图带领得克萨斯队进入射门范围，但他匆忙地将球传出，结果球直接落入捷足者队防守队员手中，造成关键的拦截。

几分钟后，俄克拉荷马队踢出一记制胜射门，以27比24获胜。

曼宁赛后说道：“我认为我们自己打败了自己。我不能随意地处理球。”

这种随意处理球的态度让得克萨斯队在整个下午都处于不利地位，他们一度领先10分，最终却遭遇了本赛季的首次失利。除了最后一分钟的拦截之外，他在第四节早些时候还一次失误，导致对方将球带回并完成达阵，从而让俄克拉荷马队首次取得领先。总而言之，曼宁的失误直接导致捷足者队获得了10分。

得克萨斯队主教练史蒂夫·萨基西安说道：“我们现在感到愤怒和沮丧。”

周六这场失利最令人费解的地方不仅仅是，全国排名第一的球队输给了一支胜率仅五成、已跌出“前25强”榜单的球队。更令人费解的是，得克萨斯队整个休赛期都在致力于打造一支完美的足球机器，围绕着这位完美的四分卫来构建球队——他们打造了一支如此精良的阵容，本不应该陷入这种生死攸关的局面。

在阿奇·曼宁上赛季作为首发球员首次亮相后，得克萨斯长角牛队未能进入季后赛，本赛季他们花费了超过 4000 万美元用于 NIL 支出，以增强球队实力。得克萨斯队大手笔地签下了多名实力派球员，包括外接手卡姆·科尔曼和跑卫好莱坞·斯莫瑟斯。他们的目标只有一个：让阿奇·曼宁不辜负曼宁家族的声誉，成为橄榄球界的传奇人物。

“当我们发挥出最佳水平时，我们是一支极具爆发力的进攻型橄榄球队，”史蒂夫·萨基西安谈到这些新球员时说道，“去年我们并没有达到这种水平。”

然而，球队的实力并未如预期般得到提升。根据 Stats Perform 的数据，阿奇·曼宁场均传球码数仅为 224.4 码，在全国排名第 53 位。他的场均传球达阵次数为 2 次，排名第 32 位，传球效率评分为 64 位。

最令人担忧的是，阿奇·曼宁无法将球准确地传给自己的队友。在上赛季仅有 7 次传球失误的情况下，他在 2026 赛季的 5 场比赛中就出现了 5 次传球失误。总的来说，他 3.4% 的传球最终落入了对手手中——这个比率高于全国其他 106 名四分卫。

周六，围绕阿奇·曼宁率领的进攻组的担忧变得非常严重，以至于史蒂夫·萨基西安决定尝试一次 53 码的射门，而不是在第四档、两码的距离时继续进攻。（这次射门没有命中。）尽管输给了他们最大的竞争对手，得克萨斯长角牛队仍然不认为情况已经到了无可挽回的地步。在季后赛扩大的时代，一次失败并不意味着他们对冠军的希望彻底破灭。

“我们还没死，”史蒂夫·萨基西安说道，“我们会没事的。我们会重新站起来。”

无论得克萨斯队的赛季最终如何，那些期待着下一位伟大的曼宁的球迷可能需要面对一个略显黯淡的现实。海斯曼奖几乎已经与他失之交臂，他在 NFL 选秀中第一顺位的位置也面临着来自其他球员的威胁，其中就包括俄克拉荷马州立大学的四分卫德鲁·梅斯特梅克。

“阿奇竭尽全力地比赛了，”史蒂夫·萨基西安说道，尽管他的几次传球失误导致得克萨斯队失去了不败的记录。“他是一位极具竞争力的球员，他希望能够弥补这些失误。”

Barely a month ago, Arch Manning finally looked like the golden boy quarterback the football world had been waiting to see. With a furious fourth-quarter rally, Manning had led Texas to a 20-point comeback win over Ohio State to launch the Longhorns to the No. 1 spot in the Top 25. He entrenched himself as the favorite to win the Heisman Trophy, guide Texas to a national title and cement himself as the top pick in the NFL draft.

But in a stunning collapse against Oklahoma on Saturday, Manning showed that years after being anointed as football's next star he remains far from the finished product.

With 20 seconds left in a tie game, Manning was trying to drive Texas into field-goal range when he slung a desperate pass off his back foot—straight into the hands of a Sooners defender for a critical interception.

Moments later, Oklahoma kicked a field goal to win 27-24.

“I think we beat ourselves,” Manning said after the game. “I can't be loose with the ball.”

That looseness doomed Texas all afternoon long, as a 10-point lead crumbled into their first defeat of the season. In addition to the last-minute interception, he threw a pick earlier in the fourth quarter that was returned for a touchdown, giving Oklahoma its first lead of the day. In all, Manning's turnovers led directly to 10 Sooner points.

“There's a level of anger and frustration,” Texas coach Steve Sarkisian said.

The puzzling thing about Saturday's meltdown wasn't simply that the No. 1 team in the nation lost to a .500 team that had dropped out of the Top 25 rankings. It was that Texas spent all offseason dedicated to building the perfect football machine around the perfect football prospect—a roster so refined they wouldn't be in this kind of do-or-die situation to begin with.

After Manning's debut as a full-time starter last season ended without a playoff appearance, the Longhorns shelled out more than $40 million in NIL spending on this year's roster. Texas splurged on high-profile transfers such as receiver Cam Coleman and running back Hollywood Smothers. They had one job: Make Arch Manning live up to his last name and join football royalty.

“When we're at our best, we're a very explosive offensive football team,” Sarkisian said of the additions. “We were not that a year ago.”

The dynamite hasn't quite materialized. Manning has thrown for just 224.4 yards per game, the 53st-highest average in the country, according to Stats Perform. His two touchdowns per game are 32nd, and his passer rating is 64th.

Most worrisome of all is Manning's inability to throw the football to his own team. After throwing seven interceptions all of last season, he has racked up five in just five games in 2026. In all, 3.4% of his throws have ended up in the hands of his opponents—a higher rate than 106 other quarterbacks nationwide.

The concerns around the Manning-led attack became so severe on Saturday that Sarkisian elected to try a 53-yard field goal rather than go for it on 4th-and-2. (The kick missed.) But despite a crushing loss to their biggest rivals, the Longhorns don't believe the sky is falling. In the age of an expanded playoff, one defeat doesn't spell doom for championship hopes.

“We're not dead,” Sarkisian said. “We're going to be OK. We'll get back up off the mat.”

However Texas' season ends up, fans awaiting the next great Manning may have to face a somewhat duller reality. The Heisman Trophy has all but slipped from his grasp, and his hold on the No. 1 spot in the NFL draft is facing threats from, among others, upstart Oklahoma State quarterback Drew Mestemaker.

“Arch competed his tail off,” Sarkisian said, despite the throws that cost Texas its unbeaten record. “The competitor in him, he'd love to have that back.”

阿奇·曼宁被抄截（照片说明）

P3 · 延伸

阿奇·曼宁（上图）在第四节比赛中两次被对方截球，其中一次被佩顿·鲍文（下图）截球并直接冲刺得分，最终球队以27比24的比分输掉了比赛。（上图：杰弗里·麦克沃特/美联社；萨拉·迪金斯/《圣安东尼奥快报》/美联社。）

A18

那位在 Planet Fitness 跑步机上进行训练的马拉松运动员

P2 · 重点

Rachel Bachman

顶尖马拉松运动员会竭尽全力，追求最佳成绩，为此他们不惜花费巨资，聘请一流教练，并在高海拔地区进行训练，以获得优势。

文尼·毛里将他的钱花在了Planet Fitness的会员资格上。这位25岁，居住在俄亥俄州扬斯敦市郊的选手，自己进行训练，并每月支付24.99美元，在当地健身房的标准跑步机上完成大部分训练里程。

“我甚至都没想到跑步机能达到这么快的速度！”文尼·毛里的一位Strava粉丝在一篇关于他最近一次跑步的帖子中评论道。

文尼·毛里这种非常规的训练方法已经开始显现效果。今年四月，他在托莱多玻璃城马拉松中获胜，震惊了跑步界，以2小时5分钟54秒的成绩打破了赛道纪录。

这意味着他以每英里4分48秒的速度跑完了26.2英里——这是美国选手有史以来跑出的最快的马拉松首秀成绩。上周日，这位跑步机上的奇迹选手参加了芝加哥马拉松，面对更加强大和具有挑战性的竞争对手，最终以2小时6分45秒的成绩获得第十名。

“人们喜欢我能在这里完成训练，在家完成训练，”文尼·毛里说，他经常在健身房遇到以前的高中同学。“我不需要搬到其他地方，也能做我喜欢的事情。”

大多数长跑运动员会因为要在如此单调的机器上进行训练而感到厌烦，这种机器甚至被戏称为“令人恐惧的跑步机”。（这种机器的早期原型曾被用作19世纪英国监狱中的惩罚工具。）但对于文尼·毛里来说，跑步机远不止是一种最后的训练工具，它实际上激发了他的运动生涯的复兴。

在俄亥俄州沃伦市的豪兰高中就读时，文尼·毛里赢得了3200米赛跑的州冠军。但在大学里，他先是在亚利桑那州立大学，然后作为研究生在圣母大学就读，表现并不出色。他在比赛中几次遭遇不幸，并受到持续的伤病困扰。

他说，问题在于他太喜欢跑步了。他会对训练感到非常兴奋，结果常常过度训练。

大学毕业后，他发现很难将生物医学工程学位运用到实际工作中，于是他把目光转向了马拉松。跑步机让他得以逃离俄亥俄州的寒冬，但他很快发现了跑步机还有其他优点。

跑步机的表面比路面更有弹性，对他的身体也更友好，避免了他在硬地上跑步时产生的冲击。它还能避免他进行转弯，这对于他大学时期一直存在的髋部伤势来说，也是一种缓解。

最重要的是，跑步机就像一个节拍器，帮助他保持稳定的节奏，并抑制他过度训练的冲动。

克里斯·尼尔说，他在圣母大学担任文尼·毛里的教练时，文尼偶尔会给他发短信，说：“我今天在外面跑了，跑得有点快。”“他知道自己需要一个速度控制装置来约束自己的跑步。”

但这并不意味着文尼·毛里会放慢速度。他史诗般的跑步机训练已经成为跑步爱好者中的传奇。六月份，他在跑步机上跑完了一场马拉松，时间大约是2小时20分钟。最近，他还在一天内通过两次跑步机训练，跑了令人难以置信的45英里。他已经将跑步机用于本周日马拉松训练的大约70%的里程。

文尼·毛里说：“在室内，天气总是好的。”

至于单调感，文尼·毛里已经形成了一套完整的训练方法。在开始跑步后，他会把一件T恤盖在数字显示屏上，这样他就不会被诱惑去调整设置。“这在我放空时有点帮助，”他说。

为了打发时间，文尼·毛里会浏览 YouTube，听音乐或单口喜剧，或者看高尔夫比赛。他还喜欢在跑步机上直接给朋友打电话，“就像和我们闲聊一样，即使他在以 4 分 40 秒的配速进行一项持续 50 分钟的疯狂训练，”泰勒·克拉克说道，他曾是扬斯敦州立大学的跑步运动员，也是文尼·毛里的偶尔一起训练的伙伴。

文尼·毛里最近才转为职业选手，并与亚瑟士签订了运动鞋赞助协议。不过，在取得突破性马拉松成绩之后，他获得了一项价值可能不如赞助协议，但可能更有用的福利：一张为期一年的 Planet Fitness 健身房免费会员卡。

Elite marathoners go to extreme lengths to chase top times, shelling out fortunes on fancy coaching and remote altitude camps to gain an edge.

Vinny Mauri spends his money on a Planet Fitness membership. The 25-year-old who lives outside Youngstown, Ohio, is self-coached and logs most of his mileage on standard treadmills at his local gym for $24.99 a month.

“Didn't even think those could go that fast!” one of Mauri's Strava's followers commented on a recent run.

Mauri's unconventional approach is already paying off. He shocked the running world when he won Toledo's Glass City Marathon in April, shattering the course record with a finish of 2 hours, 5 minutes and 54 seconds.

That's 4:48 a mile for 26.2 miles—the fastest debut marathon ever recorded by an American. On Sunday, the treadmill phenom took on a much larger and tougher field at the Chicago Marathon and finished 10th, in 2:06:45.

“People love that I'm just doing it from here, doing it from home,” said Mauri, who often bumps into former high-school classmates at the gym. “Didn't really have to relocate to do the thing that I love.”

Most distance runners would shudder at the thought of grinding away on a machine so boring it has earned the nickname “dreadmill.” (An early ancestor of the machine was used as punishment in 19th-century British prisons.) But for Mauri, the treadmill turned out to be far more than the workout machine of last resort. It actually sparked his athletic rebirth.

As a senior at Howland High in Warren, Ohio, Mauri won the 3,200-meter state title. But in college, first at Arizona State then as a graduate student at Notre Dame, he underachieved. He had a few bad-luck stumbles in races, and suffered from nagging injuries.

The problem, he said, was that he loved running too much. He would get so excited about workouts that he often overdid it.

After college, when he struggled to put his degree in biomedical engineering to good use, he set his sights on the marathon. The treadmill offered an escape from Ohio's winters, but he quickly found other advantages.

The treadmill surface is bouncier than the road, more forgiving on his body than pounding on pavement. It also saves him from making turns, which is easier on a nagging hip injury he carried in college.

And most importantly, the machine is like a metronome, keeping him steady and curbing his impulse to go overboard.

“Every once in a while he'll text me and be like, ‘I ran outside today. Got a little fast,’” said Chris Neal, who coached Mauri at Notre Dame. “He knows that he kind of needs that speed governor on his running.”

Which isn't to say Mauri takes it easy. His epic treadmill sessions have become legendary among running junkies. In June, he ran a full marathon on the treadmill in about 2:20. More recently, he ran a staggering 45 miles across two treadmill sessions in a single day. He's used the apparatus for about 70% of his training mileage for Sunday's marathon.

“The weather's always good inside,” Mauri said.

As for the monotony, Mauri has a whole routine. After he sets the treadmill, he throws a T-shirt over the digital display so he won't be tempted to fiddle with it. “It kind of helps when you zone out,” he said.

To pass the time, Mauri surfs YouTube, listens to music or stand-up comedy, or watches golf. He also likes to call friends right from the treadmill, “and just like shoot the breeze with us when he's doing some ridiculous 50-minute workout at a 4:40 pace,” said Tyler Clark, a former runner at Youngstown State and occasional training partner.

Mauri only recently turned pro, signing a shoe deal with Asics. After his breakout marathon, though, he received a perk that's less valuable but might be even more useful: a free one-year membership to Planet Fitness.

文尼·毛里于四月份赢得了托莱多“玻璃城马拉松”比赛，成绩为2小时05分54秒。（图片来源：慈悲健康玻璃城马拉松）

艺术评论

ARTS IN REVIEW · 3 stories

A14

灯光、摄影机、服装！

P2 · 重点

Laura Jacobs (Exhibition Review)

随着默片作为一种艺术形式的出现，时尚在银幕上扮演了至关重要且充满活力的角色。

As silent movies emerged as an art form, fashion played an essential, energizing role on screen

纽约。派拉蒙公司1927年的默片《It》的故事，由英国作家埃莉诺·格林提供，她用这个词来形容一种“吸引力”，能够吸引他人。在影片中饰演主角的21岁女演员克拉拉·鲍，从此被称为第一位“It Girl”。魅力？光彩？并非完全如此。她那白皙的脸颊、凌乱的波波头和明亮勾人的眼睛，给画面带来一丝本我气息和阵阵活力。而活力正是默片所追求的。照片是静止的，戏剧能说话，而默片是动作——“It”就是一切。

默片这种新兴的艺术形式很快开始依赖于能够吸引眼球的时尚，这并不令人意外。格林的大姐，达夫-戈登夫人，也被称为时装设计师Lucile，立即与一些女演员建立了联系，例如比莉·伯克。早在1913年，她就开始为她们的电影设计令人惊艳的礼服，这反过来也为她的时装品牌带来了声誉。令人惊讶的是，《机器中的女神：美国默片中的时尚》，在纽约的巴德研究生中心举办，是首次对这一主题进行深入探讨的大型展览。

1896年，随着托马斯·爱迪生Vitascope电影放映机在纽约首映，默片震惊了观众。然而，这种完全依赖视觉效果的媒介也带来了明确的挑战。“为了寻找在没有声音对话的情况下创作叙事的方法，”展览的介绍文字写道，“电影制作人利用服装来传达人物性格、推动情节发展并增强情感。”

最初的服装是由裁缝和时装设计师制作的，但随后出现了一批专业的服装师，他们意识到电影中的服装必须引人入胜、充满活力——它们本身就是故事的一部分。

展览的两位策展人——米歇尔·菲纳莫尔，一位研究时尚和设计的历史学家，以及艾玛·科马克，巴德研究生中心的展览和藏品研究助理策展人——提醒我们，在电影业起于东海岸、于20世纪10年代中期迁往加州的那些早期岁月里，服装并未被保存下来。即使在有声电影出现之后，在有声电影时代，它们也被反复使用和修改，而没有考虑到其历史价值。莱斯利·卡伦曾经告诉我，她在1958年的《吉吉》中穿着的令人难忘的白色礼服，由塞西尔·比顿设计，肩部装饰着燕子的翅膀，被反复使用，羽毛被拔掉，最终甚至有了袖子。那些保存下来的服装，通常是经过修复的，非常罕见，而且很少被展出。

因此，这次展览呈现了16件保存下来的默片时期的珍品，以及静帧和电影片段，展示了这些服装在电影中的实际效果，堪称一次丰盛的收获。其中，有两套莉莲·吉什在D.W.格里菲斯的《一路向东》（1920年）中穿着的服装；一套大胆的特拉维斯·班顿设计的晚礼服，同时被用于多萝西·阿兹纳的《女性时尚》（1927年）和A.爱德华·萨瑟兰的《尖头高跟鞋》（1929年）；以及一件流苏淡紫色礼服——葛洛丽亚·斯旺森在塞西尔·B·戴米尔《男人与女人》（1919年）中从这件礼服里蜕身而出。这些珍品被周围大量的同时期服装、配饰、艺术品、海报、草图、照片和50多部默片片段所环绕。

这次展览展出了超过180件展品，分布在三个楼层，第一层展示了早期电影体验。一件1920年代天蓝色黑边引座员制服（可能来自伦敦广场剧院）立在入口处。视频展示了人们在新“电影宫殿”中的场景，在那里，不同性别和阶级的人们聚集在一起，并发生了一些有趣的冲突。展示了色彩鲜艳的玻璃幻灯片、如《Photoplay》之类的粉丝杂志，以及印有明星脸（宝拉·内格里！比比·丹尼尔斯！）的Beautebox锡盒，这些都展示了针对电影观众的各种广告和收藏品。

二楼聚焦那些找Callot姐妹、Madame Frances和亨利·本德尔定制戏服的女明星，再往下是新制片厂制度及其设计师：他们从黑白光谱中提炼色彩感，善用反光面料——金银丝织物、天鹅绒、缎子、亮片——营造银幕上的流光溢彩。默片可能是滑稽喜剧的故乡，但它也类似于芭蕾舞。光线强化了姿势，阴影则勾勒出表情。

在第三层，重点是动作，它通过舞厅和爵士乐俱乐部场景，以及驾驶、游泳、骑马和驾驶飞机的场景，被“编织到电影的结构中”。一件保存得惊人完好的金丝雀黄礼服，为梅·默里在《女巫西茜》（1924）中闪亮定制，旁边配着她穿着它在夜总会热舞的电影片段。看看背后的那些瓦托式肩带，上面缀满了莱茵石，它们弯曲、摇曳，放大了动态效果。在银幕上，这条裙子呈现出石灰光般的亮白色。

通过精确的布局和具有象征意义的展品，策展人巧妙地将这个时代融入了中心的精致空间。毫不拖泥带水。谁能想到，展览里竟藏着一件“It”气质的圣杯之作，与鲁道夫·瓦伦蒂诺在1922年《血与沙》中穿的斗牛士夹克相邻而放。从巴黎法国电影资料馆借展、平铺在玻璃展柜中的，是一件肉色真丝雪纺吊带裙，下摆缀着银色菱形珠饰。这是1928年默片《红发》（现已佚失）的遗物，为克拉拉·鲍定制。

《机器中的女神：美国默片中的时尚》。巴德研究生中心，展至2027年1月3日。

雅各布斯女士是《Air Mail》通讯中“艺术情报报告”的编辑。

New York. The story for Paramount's 1927 silent movie “It” was provided by the British writer Elinor Glyn, who used the word to mean a “magnetic force” that draws others. The 21-year-old actress Clara Bow, who starred in the film, was henceforth known as the first “It Girl.” Charisma? Glamour? Not quite. Her creamy cheeks, tousled bob and bright, beguiling eyes brought a hint of id, jolts of energy, into the picture. And energy was what moving pictures were all about. Where photographs were still, and theater could talk, silent movies were action and “It” was everything.

That the mute infant art form soon relied on fashions that excited the eye should come as no surprise. Glyn's older sister, Lady Duff-Gordon, otherwise known as the couturier Lucile, immediately forged relationships with actresses—Billie Burke, for instance. As early as 1913 she was designing heart-stopping gowns for their movies, which in turn brought renown to her fashion house. What is surprising is that “Goddesses in the Machine: Fashion in American Silent Film,” at New York's Bard Graduate Center, is the first major exhibition to explore the subject.

Launched in the U.S. in 1896, with the New York premiere of Thomas Edison's Vitascope film projector, silent movies astonished audiences. And yet the medium, entirely visual, posed definite challenges. “Seeking ways to craft narratives without audible dialogue,” reads the show's introductory wall text, “filmmakers used costume to convey character, drive plot, and heighten emotion.”

The first costumes were made by dressmakers and couturiers, but a network of costume professionals would emerge, designers who realized that clothing in film had to be fascinating, energized—stories within the story.

The show's co-organizers—Michelle Finamore, a fashion and design historian, and Emma Cormack, the associate curator for exhibitions and study collection curator at Bard Graduate Center—remind us that during those early decades, when the movie industry began on the East Coast, and relocated to California in the mid-1910s, costumes were not preserved. Even after the advent of the talkies, and well into the sound era, they were reused and revised with no thought to historical value. Leslie Caron once told me that the unforgettable white gown she wore in 1958's “Gigi,” designed by Cecil Beaton and ravishing with swallow's wings at the shoulders, was used on and on, feathers plucked, and that it eventually had sleeves. Garments that have lasted, usually aided by conservation, are rare—and rarely exhibited.

So it's quite the bounty that the exhibition presents 16 surviving silent-screen treasures along with stills and film clips that show the pieces in action. Among them are two ensembles worn by Lillian Gish in D.W. Griffith's “Way Down East” (1920); a risqué Travis Banton evening dress used in both Dorothy Arzner's “Fashions for Women” (1927) and A. Edward Sutherland's “Pointed Heels” (1929); and a fringed mauve gown from which Gloria Swanson unwound herself in Cecil B. DeMille's “Male and Female” (1919). These gems are surrounded by a wealth of period garments, accessories, artworks, posters, sketches, photographs and clips from over 50 silent movies.

The exhibition of more than 180 objects unfolds over three floors, with the first bringing us into the early moviegoing experience. A 1920s usher's uniform in sky blue with black trim, likely from the Plaza Theatre in London, stands at the entrance. Videos show people at the new “picture palaces,” where genders and classes mingle and comic clashes ensue. Displays of brightly colored glass preview slides, fanzines such as Photoplay, and Beautebox tins lithographed with the faces of stars (Pola Negri! Bebe Daniels!) illustrate the various forms of advertising and collectibles aimed at filmgoers.

On the second floor, the curators look at the actresses who went to Callot Soeurs, Madame Frances and Henri Bendel for their costumes, and then move on to the new studio system and its designers, who pulled a sense of color from the black-and-white spectrum, and exploited reflective materials—lamé, panné velvet, satin, sequins—for onscreen shimmer and flash. Silent movies may be the home of slapstick, but they are also akin to ballet. Light reinforces gesture and shadows contour expression.

On the third floor, the focus is movement, “woven into the fabric of cinema” through dance-hall and jazz-club settings, as well as in scenes of driving, swimming, riding and piloting. A surprisingly immaculate canary-yellow dress, glitteringly embellished for Mae Murray in “Circe the Enchantress” (1924), is flanked by the film that has her wearing it in a nightclub, dancing up a storm. Look at those Watteau straps in the back, dripping with rhinestones and looping, swinging, amplifying the dynamics. Onscreen the dress is the color of limelight.

With precise plotting and symbolic pieces, the curators have neatly fit the era into the center's jewel-box spaces. Nothing is belabored. And who would have guessed that a grail of It-ness would be part of the show, nestled next to the matador jacket worn by Rudolph Valentino in 1922's “Blood and Sand.” On loan from La Cinémathèque Française, Paris, laid flat in a vitrine, is a flesh-colored silk-chiffon slip dress, its hem beaded with silver lozenges. A relic from “Red Hair,” a 1928 silent film now lost, it was made for Clara Bow.

Goddesses in the Machine: Fashion in American Silent Film. Bard Graduate Center, through Jan. 3, 2027.

Ms. Jacobs is the Arts Intel Report editor for the newsletter Air Mail.
